\textup{M}_{N}
\textup{M}_{N}
\ket{0}_{\textup{M}_{N}}
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
\textup{S}_{1}
\textup{S}_{m}
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
\matholdcal{E}^{N}

Figure 7
:
A
\matholdcal{C}^{sys+anc}
-form standard quantum circuit where each measurement in
Figure

6
is purified to a unitary using an ancilla. Here
\mathtt{O}_{i}
is the set of possible non-trivial values of the outcome
a_{i}
. The measurements
\text{$\matholdcal{M}$}^{\textup{M}_{i}}:=\{\pi^{\textup{M}_{i}}_{a_{i}}\}_{a_{i}}
applied in the global future, implement the isomorphic projective measurement of the ancilla
\textup{M}_{i}
, as do the measurements
\text{$\matholdcal{M}$}^{\textup{A}_{i}}
(from
Figure

6
) on the subset
\mathtt{S}_{i}\subseteq\mathtt{S}:=\{\textup{S}_{1},...,\textup{S}_{m}\}
of systems. It is immediate to see (and well-known) that the present circuit and that of
Figure

6
are operationally equivalent.

These two forms of circuits are illustrated in
Figure

6
and
Figure

7
.

Theorem E.1

(Recovering standard quantum circuits)
.

If an EWFS corresponds to a standard quantum scenario (
Definition

VI.3
), then its augmented circuit can be
equivalently reduced to a standard quantum circuit, such that the same (non-trivial) predictions are obtained from the original augmented circuit, the
\matholdcal{C}^{sys}
-form standard circuit or the
\matholdcal{C}^{sys+anc}
-form standard circuit. Explicitly, for any disjoint sets
\vec{a}_{j}=(a_{j_{1}},...,a_{j_{p}})
and
\vec{a}_{l}=(a_{l_{1}},...,a_{l_{q}})
of outcomes, and any choice of settings
\vec{x}=\vec{\xi}
such that
x_{i}=1
for all
i\in\{j_{1},...,j_{p},l_{1},...,l_{q}\}
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}P_{aug}(\vec{a}_{j}=\vec{\mathcal{a}}_{j}|\vec{a}_{j}=\vec{\mathcal{a}}_{j},\vec{x}=\vec{\xi})=P_{std}(\vec{a}^{\prime}_{j}=\vec{\mathcal{a}}_{j}|\vec{a}^{\prime}_{j}=\vec{\mathcal{a}}_{j}),\end{split}

(47)

where the
P_{aug}
refers to setting-conditioned predictions in the augmented circuit of the EWFS and
P_{std}
refers to predictions in an equivalent
\matholdcal{C}^{sys}
-form or
\matholdcal{C}^{sys+anc}
-form standard quantum circuit (where no settings are involved).

A proof of this theorem can be found in
Appendix

J
.

Appendix F
Detailed analysis of the FR experiment

F.1
Entanglement version of the FR experiment

In the main text, we provided a brief overview of the entanglement version of the FR scenario as well as a simple explanation of our resolution of the paradox. Having reviewed this scenario in detail in
Section

C.2
, we now provide a more detailed analysis of the same, showing the explicit calculation for every prediction involved.

We reproduce each of the individual statements used in the reasoning of the FR scenario described in
Section

C.2
, i.e., those captured by
Equation

27
-
Equation

29
. Additionally, this reasoning occurs only in a round where the super-agents observe the outcomes
u=w=\textup{ok}
, which is associated with a probability
\frac{1}{12}
in FR’s arguments as shown in
Equation

26
. We start by reproducing this probability as an explicit setting conditioning prediction in our framework. The probability of
Equation

26
is equivalent to conventional prediction
P^{FR}_{conv}(u=w=\text{ok})
(
Definition

III.6
) of the FR scenario, in computing this, FR apply the
U
assumption implicitly to model the measurements of the agents Alice and Bob purely unitarily (using
\matholdcal{M}^{A}_{unitary}
and
\matholdcal{M}^{B}_{unitary}
as seen in
Equation

26
). This corresponds precisely to the setting choices
x_{A}=x_{B}=0
(as expected from the general mapping from conventional to setting-conditioned predictions given in
Theorem

IV.1
). Therefore it follows immediately that

\displaystyle\begin{split}P(u=w=\textup{ok}|\ket{\psi}^{t=2}):=P^{FR}_{conv}(u=w=\text{ok})=P(u=w=\text{ok}|x_{A}=x_{B}=0)\end{split}

(48)

Note that in our notation for predictions, we don’t explicitly condition on the initial state of the scenario as this is taken to be in the common knowledge of all agents. See
Section

VII.1
for a discussion on how our framework and arguments can generalise to the case where one relaxes this common knowledge assumption.

Now we proceed to analysing each of the statements that agents make when the above post-selection on
u=w=\textup{ok}
succeeds. Consider the statement obtained in
Equation

27
, here Ursula, upon knowing that
u=w=ok
reasons about Bob’s outcome
b
using the state
\ket{\psi^{t=2}}_{\textup{R}\textup{A}\textup{S}\textup{B}}
and concludes that
u=w=\textup{ok}\Rightarrow b=1
. This logical statement is equivalently expressed in probabilities through the conventional prediction
P_{conv}(b=1|u=w=\textup{ok})=1
.

We can readily extract the setting choices implicit in the calculation of this probability for the FR protocol. Note that
\ket{\psi^{t=2}}
is obtained by applying
M^{\textup{A}}_{unitary}\otimes M^{\textup{B}}_{unitary}
to the initial state
\ket{\psi^{t=1}}
and in calculating the above-mentioned probability for
b=1
using the Born rule, FR apply the projector
\pi^{\textup{B}}_{1,1}=\ket{11}\bra{11}_{\textup{S}\textup{B}}
to
\ket{\psi^{t=2}}
. This precisely corresponds to the assigning
x_{2}=1
for Bob’s setting. On the other hand, they model Alice’s measurement as a purely unitary evolution (
M^{\textup{A}}_{unitary}
) as seen by Ursula in this reasoning step, therefore the setting choice used for Alice in this reasoning is
x_{1}=0
. Making these setting choices explicit, we see that this probability calculated in the FR reasoning is equivalent to the setting-conditioned prediction
P(b=1|u=w=\textup{ok},(x_{1},x_{2})=(0,1))=1
in our framework.

Indeed one can calculate this prediction from the augmented circuit of
Figure

4
for the FR protocol (using the Born rule and the well-known rule for conditional probabilities) and would obtain the same. We demonstrate this below, for further details on how the probability rule for setting-conditioned predictions in augmented circuits is derived, see
Appendix

D
.

\displaystyle\begin{split}P(b=1|u=w=\textup{ok},(x_{1},x_{2})=(0,1))=\frac{P(b=1,u=w=\textup{ok}|(x_{1},x_{2})=(0,1))}{P(u=w=\textup{ok}|(x_{1},x_{2})=(0,1))}.\end{split}

(49)

That this expression evaluates to unit probability is evident from the following calculation of the numerator and denominator of this expression for the FR protocol.

\displaystyle\begin{split}&P(b=1,u=w=\textup{ok}|(x_{1},x_{2})=(0,1))=\frac{1}{12}\\
=&|\bra{\textup{ok}}_{\textup{R}\textup{A}}\otimes\bra{\textup{ok}}_{\textup{S}\textup{B}}\Big({1}_{RA}\otimes\pi^{\textup{B}}_{x_{2}=1,b=1}\Big)\ket{\psi^{t=2}}|^{2}\\
=&|\bra{\textup{ok}}_{\textup{R}\textup{A}}\otimes\bra{\textup{ok}}_{\textup{S}\textup{B}}\Big({1}_{RA}\otimes\ket{11}\bra{11}_{\textup{S}\textup{B}}\Big)\ket{\psi^{t=2}}|^{2}.\end{split}

(50)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&P(u=w=\textup{ok}|(x_{1},x_{2})=(0,1))=\frac{1}{12}\\
=&\sum_{b\in\{0,1\}}|\bra{\textup{ok}}_{\textup{R}\textup{A}}\otimes\bra{\textup{ok}}_{\textup{S}\textup{B}}\Big({1}_{RA}\otimes\pi^{\textup{B}}_{x_{2}=1,b}\Big)\ket{\psi^{t=2}}|^{2}\\
=&\sum_{b\in\{0,1\}}|\bra{\textup{ok}}_{\textup{R}\textup{A}}\otimes\bra{\textup{ok}}_{\textup{S}\textup{B}}\Big({1}_{RA}\otimes\ket{bb}\bra{bb}_{\textup{S}\textup{B}}\Big)\ket{\psi^{t=2}}|^{2}.\end{split}

(51)

Having formalised FR’s logical statement
u=w=\textup{ok}\Rightarrow b=1
as the setting-conditioned prediction
P(b=1|u=w=\textup{ok},(x_{1},x_{2})=(0,1))=1
in our framework, we obtain the corresponding explicit version of the statement:

u=w=\textup{ok}\land(x_{1},x_{2})=(0,1)\Rightarrow b=1.

(52)

We now proceed to the next statement of the FR reasoning, given by
Equation

28
, where Ursula reasons about Bob’s reasoning of Alice through the statement
b=1\Rightarrow a=1
. This is equivalently expressed in terms of probabilities through the conventional prediction
P_{conv}(a=1|b=1)=1
.

To evaluate this probability using the Born rule as FR do, we must apply the projector
\pi^{\textup{A}}_{1,1}\otimes\pi^{\textup{B}}_{1,1}=\ket{11}\bra{11}_{\textup{R}\textup{A}}\otimes\ket{11}\bra{11}_{\textup{S}\textup{B}}
to
\ket{\psi^{t=2}}_{\textup{R}\textup{A}\textup{S}\textup{B}}
and it is evident that the implicit setting choices for Alice and Bob used here are
(x_{1},x_{2})=(1,1)
. Therefore, making this explicit, we have
P(a=1|b=1,(x_{1},x_{2})=(1,1))=1
. We can again verify this from the probability rule for our augmented circuit (which is simply the Born rule and standard conditional probability rule).

\displaystyle\begin{split}P(a=1|b=1,(x_{1},x_{2})=(1,1))=\frac{P(a=1,b=1|(x_{1},x_{2})=(1,1))}{P(b=1|(x_{1},x_{2})=(1,1))}\end{split}

(53)

That this evaluates to unit probability is immediate from the following expressions for the numerator and denominator.

\displaystyle\begin{split}&P(a=1,b=1|(x_{1},x_{2})=(1,1))=\frac{1}{3}\\
=&|\Big(\pi^{\textup{A}}_{x_{1}=1,a=1}\otimes\pi^{\textup{B}}_{x_{2}=1,b=1}\Big)\ket{\psi^{t=2}}|^{2}\\
=&|\Big(\ket{11}\bra{11}_{\textup{R}\textup{A}}\otimes\ket{11}\bra{11}_{\textup{S}\textup{B}}\Big)\ket{\psi^{t=2}}|^{2}.\end{split}

(54)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&P(b=1|(x_{1},x_{2})=(1,1))=\frac{1}{3}\\
=&|\Big({1}_{\textup{R}\textup{A}}\otimes\pi^{\textup{B}}_{x_{2}=1,b=1}\Big)\ket{\psi^{t=2}}|^{2}\\
=&|\Big({1}_{\textup{R}\textup{A}}\otimes\ket{11}\bra{11}_{\textup{S}\textup{B}}\Big)\ket{\psi^{t=2}}|^{2}.\end{split}

(55)

From this, as before, we can extract the explicit version of the logical statement.

b=1\land(x_{1},x_{2})=(1,1)\Rightarrow a=1.

(56)

We now turn to the third statement of the FR reasoning given in
Equation

29
, where Ursula reasons about Bob’s reasoning about Alice’s reasoning about Wigner, through the statement
a=1\Rightarrow w=\textup{fail}
. This equivalent probabilistic version is given by the conventional prediction
P_{conv}(w=\textup{fail}|a=1)=1
.

Analysing how FR calculate this probability using the Born rule, we see that this involves applying the projector
\pi^{\textup{A}}_{1,1}=\ket{11}\bra{11}_{\textup{R}\textup{A}}
to the state
\ket{\psi^{t=2}}
which gives us Alice’s setting
x_{1}=1
. Moreover, Bob is modelled purely unitarily here, through
M^{\textup{B}}_{unitary}
and we have
x_{2}=0
. Therefore, the explicit setting-conditioned prediction corresponding to this reasoning step of FR is
P(w=\textup{fail}|a=1,(x_{1},x_{2})=(1,0))=1
. This can be verified within our framework as follows.

\displaystyle\begin{split}P(w=\textup{fail}|a=1,(x_{1},x_{2})=(1,0))=\frac{P(w=\textup{fail},a=1|(x_{1},x_{2})=(1,0))}{P(a=1|(x_{1},x_{2})=(1,0))}\end{split}

(57)

The numerator and denominator are evaluated below, which makes it evident that the expression above evaluates to unity.

\displaystyle\begin{split}&P(w=\textup{fail},a=1|(x_{1},x_{2})=(1,0))=\frac{2}{3}\\
=&|{1}_{\textup{R}\textup{A}}\otimes\bra{\textup{fail}}_{\textup{S}\textup{B}}\Big(\pi^{\textup{A}}_{x_{1}=1,a=1}\otimes{1}_{\textup{S}\textup{B}}\Big)\ket{\psi^{t=2}}|^{2}\\
=&|{1}_{\textup{R}\textup{A}}\otimes\bra{\textup{fail}}_{\textup{S}\textup{B}}\Big(\ket{11}\bra{11}_{\textup{R}\textup{A}}\otimes{1}_{\textup{S}\textup{B}}\Big)\ket{\psi^{t=2}}|^{2}\end{split}

(58)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&P(a=1|(x_{1},x_{2})=(1,0))=\frac{2}{3}\\
=&|\Big(\pi^{\textup{A}}_{x_{1}=1,a=1}\otimes{1}_{\textup{S}\textup{B}}\Big)\ket{\psi^{t=2}}|^{2}\\
=&|\Big(\ket{11}\bra{11}_{\textup{R}\textup{A}}\otimes{1}_{\textup{S}\textup{B}}\Big)\ket{\psi^{t=2}}|^{2}\end{split}

(59)

Then the corresponding, explicit version of the logical statement is,

a=1\land(x_{1},x_{2})=(1,0)\Rightarrow w=\textup{fail}.

(60)

Therefore, we have explicitly derived all the statements in
Table

1
while highlighting the setting choices implicit in each of FR’s statements. As we have seen, making explicit these setting choices is sufficient to resolve the apparent paradox.

F.2
Prepare and measure version of the FR experiment

We have reviewed the original prepare and measure of the FR thought experiment in
Section

C.3
.
Here we show that the resolution proposed for the entanglement version is also applicable to the original (prepare and measure) version of the FR paradox, ref.
Frauchiger2018
. We will first show explicitly how original version of the FR protocol can also be modelled as an augmented circuit. Then, we will go through the reasoning of the prepare and measure version on a statement-by-statement basis of their apparent proof of their paradox. For every statement, we reveal the different settings which said statements are contingent on, but not stated by the authors of the FR paper.

The augmented circuit

The augmented circuit of the original prepare and measure version of the FR protocol is given in
Figure

8
. An equivalent version of this circuit is given in
Figure

9
, that makes the mapping to the entanglement version of the FR thought experiment more explicit.

The circuits encode the states and measurements of the original protocol in the computational basis as follows.
The initial state
\sqrt{\frac{1}{3}}\ket{\textup{heads}}_{\textup{R}}+\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}\ket{\textup{tails}}_{\textup{R}}
of the coin in
\overline{F}
’s lab (
Equation

31
) is represented in the computational basis as
\ket{\psi}_{R}=\sqrt{\frac{1}{3}}\ket{0}_{\textup{R}}+\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}\ket{1}_{\textup{R}}
, all other systems (
\overline{L}\backslash
,
S
and
L\backslash
) are initialised to
\ket{0}
. Then the measurement of
\overline{F}
corresponds to a computational basis measurement, with the outcome
r=head
identified with
r=0
and
r=\textup{tails}
identified with
r=1
. The preparation of
S
carried out by
\overline{F}
, based on the outcome of their measurement on
R
corresponds to a controlled Hadamard with the states
\ket{\downarrow}_{\textup{S}}
,
\ket{\uparrow}_{\textup{S}}
of the FR scenarios represented as
\ket{0}_{\textup{S}}
and
\ket{1}_{\textup{S}}
here.
Similarly, the measurement of
F
then also becomes a computational basis measurement with the outcome
z=+\frac{1}{2}
identified with
z=0
and
z=-\frac{1}{2}
identified with
z=1
. The projectors
\mathtt{\Pi}^{\overline{F}}_{x_{1}}
and
\mathtt{\Pi}^{F}_{x_{2}}
acting on the systems
\textup{R}\textup{$\overline{\mbox{L}}$}\backslash
and
\textup{S}\textup{L}\backslash
respectively are identical to the projectors
\mathtt{\Pi}^{A}_{x_{1}}
and
\mathtt{\Pi}^{B}_{x_{2}}
of
Equation

14
acting on the systems
R
A
and
S
B
, and the final measurements of W̄ and W are the same as the entanglement version of
Section

C.2
, i.e.,
\{\ket{\textup{ok}}_{\textup{S}\textup{L}\backslash}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\ket{00}-\ket{11})_{\textup{S}\textup{L}\backslash},\ket{\textup{fail}}_{\textup{S}\textup{L}\backslash}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\ket{00}+\ket{11})_{\textup{S}\textup{L}\backslash}\}
and similarly for
\{\ket{\overline{\textup{ok}}}_{\textup{R}\textup{$\overline{\mbox{L}}$}\backslash},\ket{\overline{\textup{fail}}}_{\textup{R}\textup{$\overline{\mbox{L}}$}\backslash}\}
.

Figure 8
:
Augmented circuit for the prepare and measure version of the FR scenario expressed in terms of the computational basis, as explained in the main text.

Figure 9
:
Equivalent version of the augmented circuit (
Figure

8
) of the prepare and measure FR protocol. Note that the blue box corresponding to
\mathtt{\Pi}^{\overline{F}}_{x_{1}}
commutes with the controlled Hadamard gate since the control is on the same basis as the measurements associated with the box. Then, it is easy to verify that the joint state of
\textup{R}\textup{$\overline{\mbox{L}}$}\backslash\textup{S}\textup{L}
just before the blue boxes (i.e., just after F’s measurement of
S
) is precisely the same state as
\ket{\psi^{t=2}}_{\textup{R}\textup{A}\textup{S}\textup{B}}
(
Equation

25
) of the entanglement version of the FR scenario, with
\textup{$\overline{\mbox{L}}$}\backslash
and
\textup{L}\backslash
playing the role of
A
and
B
.

Statement-by-statement analysis

We will now analyse their constructive proof step by step and point out what settings are required to reproduce their statements. We will use a notation which is close to, but not identical to, that of the authors to aid comparison.

The authors use the following notation to denote the statements by specific agents at particular times:
G^{t}
:“k” where
G\in\{\textup{W},\textup{$\overline{\mbox{W}}$},\textup{F},\textup{$\overline{\mbox{F}}$}\}
denotes the agent
G
making statement
k
at time
t
. Here
x_{1}
are for the measurements of
\overline{\mbox{F}}
while
x_{2}
are for F’s. Analogously to in the entanglement scenario, we will not need to consider settings for W nor
\overline{\mbox{W}}
since they are super observers. We will use italics when referring to the reasoning of the authors. What’s more we will use the same notation as the authors to specify different statements, but with an additional subscript indicating the
(x_{2},x_{1})
setting that said observer is using when making the statement in the corresponding augmented circuit:
G^{t}_{(x_{1},x_{2})}
:“k”. The setting
(0,0)
corresponds to the case where the agent is not reasoning about F nor
\overline{\mbox{F}}
’s measurement outcome. Note that the reasoning of a particular observer only depends on their choice of
(x_{1},x_{2})
settings and not on those of the other observers. Furthermore, we will say
G^{t}_{(x_{1},x_{2})}=\mathtt{correct}
if
G^{t}
holds under settings
(x_{1},x_{2})
,
G^{t}_{(x_{1},x_{2})}=\mathtt{false}
if statement
G^{t}
does not hold under settings
(x_{1},x_{2})
, and
G^{t}_{(x_{1},x_{2})}=\emptyset
if statement
G^{t}
involves reasoning about an observer’s measurement outcome when the choice
(x_{1},x_{2})
does not allow observer
G
to reason about said observer.

The first statement is made by
\overline{\mbox{F}}
at time
n\!:\!00
:
\textup{$\overline{\mbox{F}}$}^{n:00}
:“The value
w
is obtained by a measurement of L w.r.t. basis
\{\pi_{w=\textup{ok}}^{n:10},\pi_{w=\textup{fail}}^{n:10}\}
, which is completed at time
n:31
”.

Here
\pi_{w=\textup{ok}_{P}}^{n:10^{A}}
\pi_{w=\textup{ok}}^{n:10}
,
\pi_{w=\textup{fail}}^{n:10}
are the projectors onto the
\ket{{\textup{ok}}}_{\textup{L}}
,
\ket{{fail}}_{\textup{L}}
basis. This statement is correct according to the framework of this paper for all
(x_{1},x_{2})
i.e.
\textup{$\overline{\mbox{F}}$}^{n:00}_{(0,0)}=\textup{$\overline{\mbox{F}}$}^{n:00}_{(0,1)}=\textup{$\overline{\mbox{F}}$}^{n:00}_{(1,0)}=\textup{$\overline{\mbox{F}}$}^{n:00}_{(1,1)}=\mathtt{correct}
.

Then, if
\overline{\mbox{F}}
got
r=
tails in her measurement of the coin flip, she would make the statement
\textup{$\overline{\mbox{F}}$}^{n:01}
: “The spin S is in state
\ket{\rightarrow}_{\textup{S}}
at time
n\!:\!10
”.
In our formalism, this statement would also hold since
\overline{\mbox{F}}
is only reasoning about the measurement she made, we thus have
\textup{$\overline{\mbox{F}}$}^{n:01}_{(1,0)}=\textup{$\overline{\mbox{F}}$}^{n:01}_{(1,1)}=\mathtt{correct}
and
\textup{$\overline{\mbox{F}}$}^{n:01}_{(0,0)}=\textup{$\overline{\mbox{F}}$}^{n:01}_{(0,1)}=\emptyset
. Any further statements made by agent
\overline{\mbox{F}}
would have to be pre-selected on the spin being in state
\ket{\rightarrow}_{\textup{S}}
. In
Figure

5
this would correspond to the selection of branch 1 and multiplying it by
\sqrt{3/2}
to re-normalise the branch. The authors then go on to make the following claim:
\textup{$\overline{\mbox{F}}$}^{n:00}
and
\textup{$\overline{\mbox{F}}$}^{n:01}
inserted into Q imply
w=

fail
(this is claim
\textup{$\overline{\mbox{F}}$}^{n:02}
in table 3). This statement only holds if one does not take into account F’s measurement at time
n\!:\!10
. In other words,
\textup{$\overline{\mbox{F}}$}^{n:02^{A}}_{(1,0)_{P}}
\textup{$\overline{\mbox{F}}$}^{n:02}_{(1,0)}=\mathtt{correct}
,
\textup{$\overline{\mbox{F}}$}^{n:02}_{(1,1)}=\mathtt{false}
. To see this, note from
Figure

5
that their conclusion follows from noting the cancellation of the two blue
\ket{\textup{ok}}_{\textup{L}}
kets when we pre-select on branch 2. However, branch 2 is further split into sub-branches 2.1.1. and 2.1.2. via F’s measurement. This splitting causes the blue coloured kets to not cancel each other out from
\overline{\mbox{F}}
’s perspective when reasoning under settings
(x_{1},x_{2})=(1,1)
. In terms of equations, under settings
(x_{1},x_{2})=(1,1)
we would conclude that
\textup{$\overline{\mbox{F}}$}^{n:00}
and
\textup{$\overline{\mbox{F}}$}^{n:01}
imply that the probability that
w=
fail is

\displaystyle\begin{split}&P\big(w=\textup{fail}|r=\textup{tails},(x_{1},x_{2})=(1,1)\big)\\
&=\sum_{z\in\{-1/2,1/2\}}\!\!\!\!\!\!P\big(w=\textup{fail},z|r=\textup{tails},(x_{1},x_{2})=(1,1)\big)\\
&=\frac{\sum_{z\in\{-1/2,1/2\}}\textup{tr}\left[\pi_{\textup{fail}}^{\textup{W}}\pi_{z}^{\textup{F}}\pi_{\textup{tail}}^{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{F}}$}}\rho_{\textup{Fi}}\pi_{\textup{tail}}^{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{F}}$}}\pi_{z}^{\textup{F}}\right]}{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in\{-1/2,1/2\}\\
w\in\{\textup{ok},\textup{fail}\}\end{subarray}}\textup{tr}\left[\pi_{w}^{\textup{W}}\pi_{z}^{\textup{F}}\pi_{\textup{tail}}^{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{F}}$}}\rho_{\textup{Fi}}\pi_{\textup{tail}}^{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{F}}$}}\pi_{z}^{\textup{F}}\right]}\\
&=\sum_{z\in\{-1/2,1/2\}}\frac{\textup{tr}\left[\pi_{\textup{fail}}^{\textup{W}}\pi_{z}^{\textup{F}}\pi_{\textup{tail}}^{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{F}}$}}\rho_{\textup{Fi}}\pi_{\textup{tail}}^{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{F}}$}}\pi_{z}^{\textup{F}}\right]}{\textup{tr}\left[\pi_{\textup{tail}}^{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{F}}$}}\rho_{\textup{Fi}}\right]}=\frac{1}{2},\end{split}

(61)

where
\rho_{\textup{Fi}}:=U_{F}U_{\textup{spin}}U_{\overline{F}}\rho_{0}(U_{F}U_{Spin}U_{\overline{F}})^{\dagger}
with
\rho_{0}:=\ket{\psi_{0}}\!\!\bra{\psi_{0}}
,
\ket{\psi_{0}}
being the initial state (i.e. l.h.s. of
Equation

34
). The unitaries
U_{F}
,
U_{\textup{spin}}
,
U_{\overline{F}}
are those of the protocol (see
Figure

5
) and give rise to the final state
\rho_{\textup{Fi}}=\ket{\psi_{\textup{Fi}}}\!\!\bra{\psi_{\textup{Fi}}}
with
\ket{\psi_{\textup{Fi}}}
given by the r.h.s. of
Equation

34
. F’s measurement outcome at time
n\!:00
is denoted by
r
(with
a
denoting the corresponding random variable) and
\{\pi_{z}^{\textup{F}}\}_{z\in\{-1/2,1/2\}}
is the PVM of F’s measurement at time
n\!:\!10
. We note that in the notation of the general framework of
Section

III
, these projectors would be explicitly written as
\{\pi_{1,z}^{\textup{F}}\}_{z\in\{-1/2,1/2\}}
as they correspond to the case of choosing the setting to be 1 for that measurement. In order to avoid clutter, here and in the following, we drop the setting subscript “1” in all such projectors as the meaning is evident from the context of the protocol at hand. In
Equation

61
we have used the fact that the unitary transformations taking us from the initial state to the final state commute with the measurement projectors, i.e. we have used the equivalence between the circuits of Figures
8
and
9
.

Since this probability in
Equation

61
is less than one, we can conclude that
\textup{$\overline{\mbox{F}}$}^{n:02}_{(1,1)}=\mathtt{false}
.
Now it is F’s turn to make a statement.
\textup{F}^{n:10}
: “The value
z
is obtained by a measurement of spin S with respect to
\{\pi_{z=-1/2}^{\textup{n:10}},\pi_{z=1/2}^{\textup{n:10}}\}
, which is completed at time
n\!:\!11
”.
This statement clearly holds for all setting choices for F in which F can reason about her outcome, since it is merely stating one of the rules of the protocol:
\textup{F}^{n:10}_{(0,1)}=\textup{F}^{n:10}_{(1,1)}=\mathtt{correct}
. They now further go on to state:
Suppose now that F observed
z=1/2
in round
n
. Since
\braket{\downarrow|\pi_{z=-1/2}^{n:10}|\downarrow}=1
, it follows from Q that S was not in state
\ket{\downarrow}_{\textup{S}}
, and hence that the random value
r
was not heads. Therefore
\textup{F}^{n:12}
:“I am certain that
\overline{\mbox{F}}
knows that
r
= tails at time
n\!:\!11
”.
Here F is reasoning about both her measurement outcome and that of
\overline{\mbox{F}}
. Therefore, by definition, this statement only makes sense when F chooses setting
(x_{1},x_{2})=(1,1)
since in the case
(x_{1},x_{2})=(0,1)
, F cannot reason about
\overline{\mbox{F}}
’s measurement outcome since there is no classical outcome to assign to it. We therefore have
\textup{F}^{n:12}_{(0,1)}=\textup{F}^{n:12}_{(1,0)}=\emptyset
.
Meanwhile, the following equation verifies that
\textup{F}^{n:12}_{(1,1)}=\mathtt{correct}
. Using
Equation

41
, if we post-select on F getting
z=1/2
in round
n
, then we find that the probability that
\overline{\mbox{F}}
got tails is one:

\displaystyle\begin{split}P\big(r=\textup{tails}|z=1/2,(x_{1},x_{2})=(1,1)\big)=\frac{\textup{tr}[\pi_{1/2}^{\textup{F}}\pi_{\textup{tail}}^{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{F}}$}}\rho_{\textup{Fi}}\pi_{\textup{tail}}^{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{F}}$}}]}{\sum_{r\in\{\textup{tails},\textup{heads}\}}\textup{tr}[\pi_{1/2}^{\textup{F}}\pi_{r}^{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{F}}$}}\rho_{\textup{Fi}}\pi_{r}^{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{F}}$}}]}=1,\end{split}

(62)

where
\pi_{\textup{tails}}^{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{F}}$}}=\ket{\overline{t}}\!\!\bra{\overline{t}}_{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{L}}$}}
,
\pi_{heads}^{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{F}}$}}=\ket{\overline{h}}\!\!\bra{\overline{h}}_{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{L}}$}}
are the projectors onto the lab of
\overline{\mbox{F}}
, corresponding to the two outcomes of the coin toss. The last equality follows from noting that
\textup{tr}[\pi_{1/2}^{\textup{F}}\pi_{\textup{heads}}^{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{F}}$}}\rho_{\textup{Fi}}\pi_{\textup{heads}}^{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{F}}$}}]=0
.

Similarly, the above equation can also be concluded from
Figure

5
by noting that if we are in branch 2.1.2 (this corresponds to F getting outcome
z=1/2
), then the only measurement outcome of
\overline{\mbox{F}}
which leads to this branch is
r
= tails.

The authors of the FR paradox then claim:
Therefore from
\textup{F}^{n:12}
and invoking Q, we conclude
\textup{F}^{n:13}
: “I am certain that
\overline{\mbox{F}}
is certain that W will observe
w
=
fail
at time
n\!:\!31
.”
. Statements
\{\textup{F}^{n:13}_{(x_{1},x_{2})}\}_{x_{1},x_{2}}
do not correspond to a single statement in our framework since
\textup{F}^{n:13}
is a concatenation of two statements “upon observing
z=1/2
, I know with certainty that
r=\textup{tails}
” made by F and “upon observing
r=\textup{tails}
, I know with certainty that
w=\textup{fail}
” made by
\overline{F}
. Note that these two statements are precisely FR’s
\textup{F}^{n:12}
and
\textup{$\overline{\mbox{F}}$}^{n:02}
respectively and they are combined using the assumptions
C
and
D
to give
\textup{F}^{n:13}
. Moreover, as we have seen
that
\textup{$\overline{\mbox{F}}$}^{n:02}_{(1,0)}=\mathtt{correct}
,
\textup{$\overline{\mbox{F}}$}^{n:02}_{(1,1)}=\mathtt{false}
and
\textup{F}^{n:12}_{(0,1)}=\emptyset
and
\textup{F}^{n:12}_{(1,1)}=\mathtt{correct}
, hence there is no common setting
(x_{1},x_{2})
for which both
\textup{$\overline{\mbox{F}}$}^{n:02}
and
\textup{F}^{n:12}
are correct. Hence there are no setting choice under which
\textup{F}^{n:13}
can be derived from
\textup{F}^{n:12}
and
\textup{$\overline{\mbox{F}}$}^{n:02}
as FR do. Agent F can always inherit both statements via the knowledge operator
Equation

12
, in a similar way to how you, the reader, is “inheriting” all the statements in this article when you read them. This, however, by itself poses little value due to the setting mismatch.

Alternatively, one can attempt a more direct derivation of the prediction associated with
\textup{F}^{n:13}
, which tells us something about the outcome
w=
fail based on the observation of the outcome
z=1/2
. This requires the setting
x_{2}=1
.
Then, from F’s perspective under settings
(x_{1},x_{2})=(1,1)
and after obtaining measurement outcome
z=1/2
, she would conclude that the probability of
w
= fail is only

\displaystyle\begin{split}&P\big(w=\textup{fail}|z=1/2,(x_{1},x_{2})=(1,1)\big)\\
&=\sum_{r\in\{\textup{heads},\textup{tails}\}}P\big(r|z=1/2,(x_{1},x_{2})=(1,1)\big)P\big(w=\textup{fail}|r,z=1/2,(x_{1},x_{2})=(1,1)\big)\\
&=P\big(w=\textup{fail}|r=\textup{tails}\,\&\,z=1/2,(x_{1},x_{2})=(1,1)\big)\\
&=\frac{\textup{tr}[\pi_{\textup{fail}}^{\textup{W}}\pi_{1/2}^{\textup{F}}\pi_{\textup{tails}}^{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{F}}$}}\rho_{\textup{Fi}}\pi_{\textup{tails}}^{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{F}}$}}\pi_{1/2}^{\textup{F}}]}{\sum_{w\in\{\textup{ok},\textup{fail}\}}\textup{tr}[\pi_{w}^{\textup{W}}\pi_{1/2}^{\textup{F}}\pi_{\textup{tails}}^{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{F}}$}}\rho_{\textup{Fi}}\pi_{\textup{tails}}^{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{F}}$}}\pi_{1/2}^{\textup{F}}]}\\
&=\frac{\textup{tr}[\pi_{\textup{fail}}^{\textup{W}}\pi_{1/2}^{\textup{F}}\pi_{\textup{tails}}^{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{F}}$}}\rho_{\textup{Fi}}\pi_{\textup{tails}}^{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{F}}$}}\pi_{1/2}^{\textup{F}}]}{\textup{tr}[\pi_{1/2}^{\textup{F}}\pi_{\textup{tails}}^{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{F}}$}}\rho_{\textup{Fi}}\pi_{\textup{tails}}^{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{F}}$}}]}=\frac{1}{2}<1,\end{split}

(63)

where we have used
Equation

62
and the last inequality follows from noting that if we are on branch 2.1.2., then the possibility of W measuring fail cannot be one since he can get outcome ok too (since due to F’s measurement, the blue cancellation does not take place). Note also that the same probability is obtained under the settings
(x_{1},x_{2})=(0,1)
.

The authors now proceed to reason from the perspective of the super-observers. The first statement is
\textup{$\overline{\mbox{W}}$}^{n:21}
: “System R is initialised to
\ket{\textup{init}}_{\textup{R}}
at time
n\!:\!00
.
This statement is true for all settings, since it is a statement about the protocol, which all agents are assumed to know, so
\textup{$\overline{\mbox{W}}$}^{n:21}_{(0,1)}=\textup{$\overline{\mbox{W}}$}^{n:21}_{(1,0)}=\textup{$\overline{\mbox{W}}$}^{n:21}_{(1,1)}=\mathtt{correct}
. The authors then point out that
the state
U_{\overline{F}}\ket{\textup{init},\phi_{0},S_{0}}
(r.h.s of
Equation

33
) is orthogonal to
\ket{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{{ok}}}$}}_{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{L}}$}}\ket{\downarrow}_{\textup{S}}
. This is indeed correct, as can be readily seen from
Figure

5
by observing that
\ket{\downarrow}\!\!\bra{\downarrow}_{\textup{S}}
projects onto the superposition of branches 1.1.1. and 2.1.1. and that for these branches, the purple
\ket{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{{ok}}}$}}_{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{L}}$}}
terms cancel each other out. The authors then state this in the form of an expectation value of a projector, namely
\braket{\textup{init}|\pi_{(\overline{w},z)\neq(\textup{$\overline{\mbox{ok}}$},-1/2)}^{n:00}|\textup{init}}=1
. Here
\pi_{(\overline{w},z)\neq(\textup{$\overline{\mbox{ok}}$},-1/2)}^{n:00}={1}-\pi_{(\overline{w},z)=(\textup{$\overline{\mbox{ok}}$},-1/2)}^{n:00}
, where
\pi_{(\overline{w},z)=(\textup{$\overline{\mbox{ok}}$},-1/2)}^{n:00}
is a Heisenberg picture projector that would first transform
\ket{\textup{init}}_{\textup{R}}
to

U_{\overline{F}}\ket{\textup{init},\phi_{0},S_{0}}
(through the appropriate isometry that appends
\ket{\phi_{0},S_{0}}_{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{L}}$}\backslash\textup{S}}
and performs
U_{\overline{F}}
) and then projects onto the outcomes
(\overline{w},z)=(\textup{$\overline{\mbox{ok}}$},-1/2)
, as
\ket{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{ok}}$}}\bra{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{ok}}$}}_{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{L}}$}}\otimes\ket{\downarrow}\bra{\downarrow}_{\textup{S}}\otimes{1}_{\textup{L}\backslash}
i.e,
\pi_{(\overline{w},z)\neq(\textup{$\overline{\mbox{ok}}$},-1/2)}^{n:00}
is the Heisenberg projector onto the complement of outcomes
(\overline{w},z)=(\textup{$\overline{\mbox{ok}}$},-1/2)
. They then claim
Agent
\overline{\mbox{W}}
, who uses Q, can hence be certain that
(\overline{w},z)\neq(\textup{$\overline{\mbox{ok}}$},-1/2)
and that this implies (when
\overline{w}
=
\overline{\mbox{ok}}
) the statement
\textup{$\overline{\mbox{W}}$}^{n:22}
: “I am certain that F knows that
z=1/2
at time
n\!:\!11
”.
Now the authors are allowing
\overline{\mbox{W}}
to take into account the measurement outcome of F in their reasoning but not the measurement of
\overline{\mbox{F}}
. In other words, they are using settings
(0,1)
and thus assigning

\displaystyle\begin{split}&P\big(z=1/2|\overline{w}=\textup{$\overline{\mbox{ok}}$},(x_{1},x_{2})=(0,1)\big)\\
&=\sum_{w\in\{\textup{ok},\textup{fail}\}}P\big(z=1/2,w|\overline{w}=\textup{$\overline{\mbox{ok}}$},(x_{1},x_{2})=(0,1)\big)\\
&=\sum_{w\in\{\textup{ok},\textup{fail}\}}\frac{\textup{tr}[\pi_{w}^{\textup{W}}\pi_{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{o}}$k}}^{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{W}}$}}\pi_{1/2}^{\textup{F}}\rho_{\textup{Fi}}\pi_{1/2}^{\textup{F}}]}{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in\{-1/2,1/2\}\\
w^{\prime}\in\{\textup{ok},\textup{fail}\}\end{subarray}}\textup{tr}[\pi_{w^{\prime}}^{\textup{W}}\pi_{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{o}}$k}}^{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{W}}$}}\pi_{z}^{\textup{F}}\rho_{\textup{Fi}}\pi_{z}^{\textup{F}}]}\\
&=\frac{\textup{tr}[\pi_{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{o}}$k}}^{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{W}}$}}\pi_{1/2}^{\textup{F}}\rho_{\textup{Fi}}\pi_{1/2}^{\textup{F}}]}{\sum_{z\in\{-1/2,1/2\}}\textup{tr}[\pi_{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{o}}$k}}^{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{W}}$}}\pi_{z}^{\textup{F}}\rho_{\textup{Fi}}\pi_{z}^{\textup{F}}]}=1,\end{split}

(64)

where the last line follows from observing that in
Figure

5
we have that
\textup{tr}[\pi_{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{o}}$k}}^{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{W}}$}}\pi_{-1/2}^{\textup{F}}\rho_{\textup{Fi}}\pi_{-1/2}^{\textup{F}}]=0
and thus the r.h.s. of above is one, in accordance with what the authors claim. We thus have
\textup{$\overline{\mbox{W}}$}^{n:22}_{(0,1)}=\mathtt{correct}
.

While they are taking into account F’s measurement while reasoning, they are not taking into account
\overline{\mbox{F}}
’s measurement when reasoning. We can check that the statement
\textup{$\overline{\mbox{W}}$}^{n:22}_{(1,1)}=\mathtt{false}
since the following probability, which takes into account both
\overline{\mbox{F}}
’s and F’s measurements, is strictly less than one:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&P\big(z=1/2|\overline{w}=\textup{$\overline{\mbox{ok}}$},(x_{1},x_{2})=(1,1)\big)\\
&=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in\{\textup{ok},\textup{fail}\}\\
r\in\{\textup{heads},\textup{tails}\}\end{subarray}}\!\!\!P\big(z=1/2,w,r|\overline{w}=\textup{$\overline{\mbox{ok}}$},(x_{1},x_{2})=(1,1)\big)\\
&=\frac{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in\{\textup{ok},\textup{fail}\}\\
r\in\{\textup{heads},\textup{tails}\}\end{subarray}}\textup{tr}[\pi_{w}^{\textup{W}}\pi_{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{o}}$k}}^{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{W}}$}}\pi_{1/2}^{\textup{F}}\pi_{r}^{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{F}}$}}\rho_{\textup{Fi}}\pi_{r}^{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{F}}$}}\pi_{1/2}^{\textup{F}}]}{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in\{-1/2,1/2\}\\
w\in\{\textup{ok},\textup{fail}\}\\
r\in\{\textup{heads},\textup{tails}\}\end{subarray}}\!\!\!\textup{tr}[\pi_{w}^{\textup{W}}\pi_{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{o}}$k}}^{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{W}}$}}\pi_{z}^{\textup{F}}\pi_{r}^{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{F}}$}}\rho_{\textup{Fi}}\pi_{r}^{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{F}}$}}\pi_{z}^{\textup{F}}]}\\
&=\frac{\sum_{r\in\{\textup{heads},\textup{tails}\}}\textup{tr}[\pi_{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{o}}$k}}^{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{W}}$}}\pi_{1/2}^{\textup{F}}\pi_{r}^{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{F}}$}}\rho_{\textup{Fi}}\pi_{r}^{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{F}}$}}\pi_{1/2}^{\textup{F}}]}{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in\{-1/2,1/2\}\\
r\in\{\textup{heads},\textup{tails}\}\end{subarray}}\textup{tr}[\pi_{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{o}}$k}}^{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{W}}$}}\pi_{z}^{\textup{F}}\pi_{r}^{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{F}}$}}\rho_{\textup{Fi}}\pi_{r}^{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{F}}$}}\pi_{z}^{\textup{F}}]}=\frac{1}{3}<1,\end{split}

(65)

Furthermore
\textup{$\overline{\mbox{W}}$}^{n:22}_{(1,0)}=\emptyset
since the statement
\textup{$\overline{\mbox{W}}$}^{n:22}
is about F’s measurement outcome. Before we move on, observe that
Equations

64
and
65
take on different values and provides another example of collider bias. In particular, these two equations show that the probability of
F
’s outcome does depend on
\overline{\mbox{F}}
’s setting
x_{1}
given the knowledge that the post-selection on
\overline{w}
succeeded. This means that the probability
P\big(z=1/2|\overline{w}=\textup{$\overline{\mbox{ok}}$}\big)
that FR consider, is not well-defined when the setting or the prior over the settings is not specified.

The authors then go on to claim
…because agent
\overline{\mbox{W}}
announces
\overline{w}
, agent W can be certain about
\overline{\mbox{W}}
’s knowledge, which justifies statement
\textup{W}^{n:26}
, where
\textup{W}^{n:26}
is (assuming
\overline{\mbox{W}}
announces
\overline{w}
=
\overline{\mbox{ok}}
at time
n\!:\!21
.) “I am certain that
\overline{\mbox{W}}
knows that
\overline{w}=

\overline{\mbox{ok}}
at time
n\!:\!21
.”
. We agree that W can be sure of any correct announcement made by
\overline{\mbox{W}}
, irrespective of their choice of
(x_{1},x_{2})
settings. Thus
\textup{W}^{n:26}_{(0,1)}=\textup{W}^{n:26}_{(1,0)}=\textup{W}^{n:26}_{(1,1)}=\mathtt{correct}
.

Next the authors make the claim
…according to quantum mechanics, agent W can be certain that the outcome (
\overline{w},w
)= (
\overline{\mbox{ok}}
,ok) occurs after finitely many rounds
. They make this based on the fact that

\displaystyle\bra{\psi_{\textup{Fi}}}\pi_{(\overline{w},w)=(\textup{$\overline{\mbox{ok}}$},\textup{ok})}\ket{\psi_{\textup{Fi}}}=\frac{1}{12},

(66)

where
\ket{\psi_{\textup{Fi}}}
is the final state given on the r.h.s. of
Equation

34
,
\pi_{(\overline{w},w)=(\textup{$\overline{\mbox{ok}}$},\textup{ok})}=\ket{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{ok}}$}}\bra{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{ok}}$}}_{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{L}}$}}\otimes\ket{\textup{ok}}\bra{\textup{ok}}_{\textup{L}}
. Note that this can also be seen as the case with
(x_{1},x_{2})=(0,0)
, as we will see later in
Equation

70
. The authors then claim
\textup{W}^{n:00}
: “I am certain that there exists a round
n
in which the halting condition at time
n\!:\!40
is satisfied.”
Noting that

\displaystyle\begin{split}&P\big(\overline{w}=\overline{\textup{ok}},w=\textup{ok}|(x_{1},x_{2})=(1,1)\big)\\
&=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in\{-1/2,1/2\}\\
r\in\{\textup{heads},\textup{tails}\}\end{subarray}}\textup{tr}[\pi_{\textup{ok}}^{\textup{W}}\pi_{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{o}}$k}}^{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{W}}$}}\pi_{z}^{\textup{F}}\pi_{r}^{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{F}}$}}\rho_{\textup{Fi}}\pi_{r}^{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{F}}$}}\pi_{z}^{\textup{F}}]\\
&=\!\!\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in\{-1/2,1/2\}\\
r\in\{\textup{heads},\textup{tails}\}\end{subarray}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\big|\!\bra{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{ok}}$}}\!\bra{\textup{ok}}\pi_{z}^{\textup{F}}U_{F}U_{Spin}\pi_{r}^{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{F}}$}}U_{\overline{F}}\ket{\textup{init},\phi_{0},S_{0}}_{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{L}}$}\textup{S}}\ket{\phi_{0}}_{\textup{L}}\!\big|=\frac{5}{6}\end{split}

(67)

is positive, we conclude that
\textup{W}^{n:00}_{(1,1)}=\mathtt{correct}
. Similarly, we observe that

\displaystyle\begin{split}P\big(\overline{w}=\overline{\textup{ok}},w=\textup{ok}|(x_{1},x_{2})=(0,1)\big)=\sum_{z\in\{-1/2,1/2\}}\textup{tr}[\pi_{\textup{ok}}^{\textup{W}}\pi_{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{ok}}$}}^{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{W}}$}}\pi_{z}^{\textup{F}}\rho_{\textup{Fi}}\pi_{z}^{\textup{F}}]=\frac{1}{2}\end{split}

(68)

in positive, thus
\textup{W}^{n:00}_{(0,1)}=\mathtt{correct}
.
Likewise, there exits
r\in\{\textup{tails},\textup{heads}\}
such that

\displaystyle\begin{split}P\big(\overline{w}=\overline{\textup{ok}},w=\textup{ok}|(x_{1},x_{2})=(1,0)\big)=\sum_{r\in\{\textup{heads},\textup{tails}\}}\textup{tr}[\pi_{\textup{ok}}^{\textup{W}}\pi_{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{ok}}$}}^{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{W}}$}}\pi_{r}^{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{F}}$}}\rho_{\textup{Fi}}\pi_{r}^{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{F}}$}}]=\frac{1}{2}\end{split}

(69)

is positive thus
\textup{W}^{n:00}_{(1,0)}=\mathtt{correct}
.
Finally,

\displaystyle\begin{split}P\big(\overline{w}=\overline{\textup{ok}},w=\textup{ok}|(x_{1},x_{2})=(0,0)\big)=\textup{tr}[\pi_{\textup{ok}}^{\textup{W}}\pi_{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{ok}}$}}^{\textup{$\overline{\mbox{W}}$}}\rho_{\textup{Fi}}]=\frac{1}{12},\end{split}

(70)

hence
\textup{$\overline{\mbox{W}}$}^{n:00}_{(0,0)}=\mathtt{correct}
. Therefore we conclude from
Equations

67
,
68
,
69
and
70
that while the statement
\textup{$\overline{\mbox{W}}$}^{n:00}_{(x_{1},x_{2})}
is correct for all settings, the value of the corresponding probability which FR calculate is not correct for all settings.

Next, the authors state
Agent F may insert agent’s
\overline{\mbox{F}}
’s statement
\textup{$\overline{\mbox{F}}$}^{n:02}
into
\textup{F}^{n:12}
, obtaining statement
\textup{F}^{n:13}
. As we pointed out above, said statement only holds under certain settings. The authors then go on to say
By virtue of [assumption]
C
, she [agent F] may then conclude that statement
\textup{F}^{n:14}
holds, too.
This statement is
\textup{F}^{n:14}
: “I am certain that W will observe
w
=
fail
at time
n\!:\!31
”.
This statement is merely the same as
\textup{F}^{n:13}
but now with the difference that F
herself
is certain that W will observe
w
= fail at time
n\!:\!31
rather that F being merely certain that
\overline{\mbox{F}}
is certain. There are no settings F can select for which this statement is true since

\displaystyle P\big(w=\textup{fail}|z=1/2,(x_{1},x_{2})=(x_{1},1)\big)<1

(71)

for all settings
x_{1}\in\{0,1\}
. Moreover, F cannot inherit the statement “I am certain that W will observe
w
= fail at time
n\!:\!31
” via the transfer of knowledge operator since this statement does not make reference to agent
\overline{\mbox{F}}
and thus it would require F
himself
to use settings
(x_{1},x_{2})=(1,0)
just after obtaining the measurement outcome
z=\frac{1}{2}
; yet the latter requires setting
x_{2}=1
(This is distinct to the case of
\textup{F}^{n:13}
).

The remaining statements made by W and
\overline{\mbox{W}}
are derived from the previous statements under the assumptions
Q
,
U
,
C
,
D
, and
S
. However, they do so disregarding the setting parameters and hence reaching their apparent contradiction.

In
Table

2
, we compare the original statements of the FR paper used in deriving the apparent contradiction and the explicit version of those statements obtained within our framework. This is analogous to
Table

1
of the entanglement case and it can be immediately seen that while the original statements (which ignore setting information) can be combined to yield the apparent contradiction, the explicit statements (which specify the setting choice) cannot be combined in this manner even using the standard rules of classical logic. These explicit statements can also be derived directly within the augmented circuit of the EWFS at hand, which is the prepare and measure version of the FR scenario. This augmented circuit is illustrated in
Figure

8
and an equivalent version of this circuit (that makes the mapping to the entanglement formulation of the scenario more evident) is given in
Figure

9
.

In summary, in this section we have shown that each of the individual statements that FR use to derive a contradiction (see
Table

2
) hold under some choice of settings in our framework (note this also follows from
Theorem

IV.1
of the general framework). However, each of these statements requires a different setting choice and can no longer be combined using the FR assumptions to yield a paradox.

with the additional implicit assumptions needed to make each statement. Without the implicit assumptions, the implications drawn from
Q
in the original FR paper are summarised in the modal logic language in column 4. The corresponding explicit version of the same implications that take the additional implicit assumptions into account are given in the last column. While original implications can be combined using
C
and the distributive axiom to yield the further implications listed in Table 3 of the FR paper, the explicit version of these implications cannot be combined even using the standard rules of classical logic such as
C
and the distributive axiom. We therefore see that the paradox never arises even when using
Q
,
C
and
S
and modelling agents unitarily as long as we are careful to use the explicit version of the implications. The variables
x
and
y
appearing in the last column are the settings appearing in the circuit of

, these encode the additional implicit assumptions.

F.3
Setting-dependence in FR’s experiment

It
Section

F.1
, we mapped each FR probability (
P_{conv}(b=1|u=w=\textup{ok})=1
,
P_{conv}(a=1|b=1)=1
and
P_{conv}(w=\textup{fail}|a=1)=1
) to a unique setting choice
(x_{1},x_{2})\in\{(1,0),(1,1),(0,1)\}
that reproduces the same probability in our framework. It is illustrative to go beyond this analysis and consider other possible setting choices we can assign to each statement. Then we find that in contrast to the above-mentioned settings which do reproduce the exact probabilities of the FR paper, alternate setting choices not longer give the same probabilities, and therefore do not yield the desired logical statements needed in FR’s reasoning. For instance, we showed in
Section

F.1
that
P_{conv}(b=1|u=w=\textup{ok})=P(b=1|u=w=\textup{ok},(x_{1},x_{2})=(0,1))=1
. We can alternatively consider
P(b=1|u=w=\textup{ok},(x_{1},x_{2})=(1,1))
. Note that we cannot consider any setting choice with
x_{2}=0
here because the prediction involves Bob’s outcome
b=1
and therefore must involve a non-trivial projector on Bob’s side in its evaluation.

\displaystyle\begin{split}&P(b=1,u=w=\textup{ok}|(x_{1},x_{2})=(1,1))=\frac{1}{12}\\
=&\sum_{a\in\{0,1\}}|\bra{\textup{ok}}_{\textup{R}\textup{A}}\otimes\bra{\textup{ok}}_{\textup{S}\textup{B}}\Big(\pi^{\textup{A}}_{x_{1}=1,a}\otimes\pi^{\textup{B}}_{x_{2}=1,b=1}\Big)\ket{\psi^{t=2}}|^{2}\\
=&\sum_{a\in\{0,1\}}|\bra{\textup{ok}}_{\textup{R}\textup{A}}\!\otimes\bra{\textup{ok}}_{\textup{S}\textup{B}}\!\Big(\ket{aa}\!\bra{aa}_{\textup{R}\textup{A}}\otimes\ket{11}\bra{11}_{\textup{S}\textup{B}}\Big)\!\ket{\psi^{t=2}}|^{2}\end{split}

(72)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&P(u=w=\textup{ok}|(x_{1},x_{2})=(1,1))=\frac{1}{4}\\
=&\sum_{a,b\in\{0,1\}}|\bra{\textup{ok}}_{\textup{R}\textup{A}}\otimes\bra{\textup{ok}}_{\textup{S}\textup{B}}\Big(\pi^{\textup{A}}_{x_{1}=1,a}\otimes\pi^{\textup{B}}_{x_{2}=1,b}\Big)\ket{\psi^{t=2}}|^{2}\\
=&\sum_{a.b\in\{0,1\}}\!\!\!|\bra{\textup{ok}}_{\textup{R}\textup{A}}\!\otimes\!\bra{\textup{ok}}_{\textup{S}\textup{B}}\!\Big(\ket{aa}\bra{aa}_{\textup{R}\textup{A}}\otimes\ket{bb}\bra{bb}_{\textup{S}\textup{B}}\Big)\!\ket{\psi^{t=2}}|^{2}\end{split}

(73)

Putting this together using the rule for conditional probabilities, we have

P(b=1|u=w=\textup{ok},(x_{1},x_{2})=(1,1))=\frac{1}{3}\neq 1

(74)

The FR probability
P_{conv}(a=1|b=1)=1
corresponds to
P(a=1|b=1,(x_{1},x_{2})=(1,1))=1
in our framework, but no alternative setting choices are possible here as the prediction refers to both Alice and Bob’s classical outcomes. Finally, the FR probability
P_{conv}(w=\textup{fail}|a=1)=1
corresponds to
P(w=\textup{fail}|a=1,(x_{1},x_{2})=(1,0))=1
in our framework and we consider the alternative setting choice
(x_{1},x_{2})=(1,1)
in this case (again
x_{1}=0
is not a possible setting choice here as the prediction involves Alice’s classical outcome). The corresponding prediction
P(w=\textup{fail}|a=1,(x_{1},x_{2})=(1,1))
is calculated below, and is also not equal to 1.

\displaystyle\begin{split}&P(a=1,w=\textup{fail}|(x_{1},x_{2})=(1,1))=\frac{1}{3}\\
=&\sum_{b\in\{0,1\}}|{1}_{\textup{R}\textup{A}}\otimes\bra{\textup{fail}}_{\textup{S}\textup{B}}\Big(\pi^{\textup{A}}_{x_{1}=1,a=1}\otimes\pi^{\textup{B}}_{x_{2}=1,b}\Big)\ket{\psi^{t=2}}|^{2}\\
=&\sum_{b\in\{0,1\}}|{1}_{\textup{R}\textup{A}}\otimes\bra{\textup{fail}}_{\textup{S}\textup{B}}\Big(\ket{11}\bra{11}_{\textup{R}\textup{A}}\otimes\ket{bb}\bra{bb}_{\textup{S}\textup{B}}\Big)\ket{\psi^{t=2}}|^{2}\end{split}

(75)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&P(a=1|(x_{1},x_{2})=(1,1))=\frac{2}{3}\\
=&\sum_{b\in\{0,1\}}|\Big(\pi^{\textup{A}}_{x_{1}=1,a=1}\otimes\pi^{\textup{B}}_{x_{2}=1,b}\Big)\ket{\psi^{t=2}}|^{2}\\
=&\sum_{b\in\{0,1\}}|\Big(\ket{11}\bra{11}_{\textup{R}\textup{A}}\otimes\ket{bb}\bra{bb}_{\textup{S}\textup{B}}\Big)\ket{\psi^{t=2}}|^{2}\end{split}

(76)

This gives us

P(w=\textup{fail}|a=1,(x_{1},x_{2})=(1,1))=\frac{1}{2}\neq 1.

(77)

In summary, we have found that
P(b=1|u=w=\textup{ok},(x_{1},x_{2})=(0,1))\neq P(b=1|u=w=\textup{ok},(x_{1},x_{2})=(1,1))
and
P(w=\textup{fail}|a=1,(x_{1},x_{2})=(1,0))\neq P(w=\textup{fail}|a=1,(x_{1},x_{2})=(1,1))
. That is the predictions do depend on the setting. This highlights that a core reason for the FR paradox in this scenario is that the FR reasoning involves ignoring the conditioning on setting values in a scenario where the predictions do depend on these value. This illustrates our general result of
Corollary

IV.2
, which shows that in any EWFS, any potential inconsistencies can only arise in this manner: ignoring the conditioning on settings, in predictions which are setting-dependent.

Reformulating this insight in a more framework-independent manner, this means that the choice of Heisenberg cuts under which we evaluate the predictions for different sets of outcomes does matter, the probabilities do depend on this choice (captured by the settings in our framework).
Our framework which explicitly takes into account the Heisenberg cut, provides a natural resolution to any such paradox without forsaking unitary quantum theory or classical logic.

Appendix G
Classical example reproducing certain features of the FR correlations

We noted earlier that the FR correlations are an example of collider bias, or in this case, they exhibit signalling under post-selection. Further, we have also seen that the assumption
\mathbf{I}
is necessary for reproducing the apparent paradox. In this section, we provide an example of a classical protocol that reproduces these features of the FR correlations. We note however that the settings in our example do not have the same physical meaning as the settings of the FR augmented circuit (which relate to the choice of Heisenberg cut). Nevertheless, the example gives an intuition for our resolution. We will thus refer to the
\mathbf{I}
applied to the settings of this classical example as
\mathbf{I}_{C}
to distinguish it from the version of the assumption applied to the settings of our augmented circuit that model Heisenberg cuts.

The augmented circuit
Figure

4
of the entanglement version of the FR protocol can be represented as shown in
Figure

10
(a).
Consider now the circuit of
Figure

10
(b), which has the same configuration as the circuit of
Figure

10
(a) for the FR protocol and consider the following classical operations in place of the boxes
\matholdcal{A}
,
\matholdcal{B}
,
\matholdcal{U}
and
\matholdcal{W}
. The initial state is simply a uniformly distributed binary random variable
\Lambda
. The operation
\matholdcal{A}
of Alice takes the given value
\lambda\in\{0,1\}
of
\Lambda
together with the binary setting
x_{1}
, and generates the outcome
a\in\{0,1\}
by taking the XOR of the two
a=\lambda\oplus x_{1}
, and forwards
a
to Ursula’s operation
\matholdcal{U}
.
\matholdcal{U}
takes
a
from Alice’s operation, internally generates a uniformly distributed bit
k_{U}\in\{0,1\}
and outputs
u=a\cdot k_{U}
where
\cdot
denotes the logical AND. Bob’s side is identical, his operation
\matholdcal{B}
takes a value of
\Lambda
along with
x_{2}
and outputs
b=\lambda\oplus x_{2}
while forwarding a copy of the classical bit
b
to Wigner’s operation
\matholdcal{W}
.
\matholdcal{W}
takes
b
from Bob, generates a uniform bit
k_{W}
internally and outputs
w=b\cdot k_{W}
.

Given this simple model, it is easy to verify that
P(a,b|x_{1},x_{2})
is non-signalling i.e.,
P(a|x_{1},x_{2})=P(a|x_{1})
and
P(b|x_{1},x_{2})=P(b|x_{2})
. However,
P(a,b|x_{1},x_{2},u)
allows signalling from Alice to Bob i.e.,
P(b|x_{1},x_{2},u)\neq P(b|x_{2},u)
and
P(a,b|x_{1},x_{2},w)
allows signalling from Bob to Alice i.e.,
P(a|x_{1},x_{2},w)\neq P(a|x_{1},w)
. This is because, for instance, conditioned on the knowledge that
u=1
(say), we know that since
u=a\cdot k_{U}
,
a=k_{U}=1
. Further, since
a=\lambda\oplus x_{1}=1
and
b=\lambda\oplus x_{2}
, we know that
b=x_{1}\oplus x_{2}\oplus 1
and Bob’s outcome
b
clearly depends on Alice’s setting
x_{1}
now. This means that when reasoning given the knowledge about some post-selections on
u
and
w
, even in classical probability theory, the agents must be careful to account for any new correlations that this may introduce between their settings, else they may end up making wrong conclusions.

Interestingly, in
Healey2018
Healey pointed out that whether or not Bob’s state is collapsed influences the probabilities of Alice’s outcome in the FR scenario due to the post-selection. Denying this dependence is what
Healey2018
refers to as the assumption of
intervention insensitivity
. Healey argues that FR implicitly assume intervention insensitivity in order obtain the logical contradiction. While Healey’s intervention sensitivity may seem similar in spirit to the setting-dependence in our framework,
Healey2018
appears to regard intervention sensitivity as a special non-local feature of quantum theory.
In the FR case, the setting-dependence takes the particular form of enabling signalling under post-selection that we have explained here. However, the present classical example shows that this is not a special feature of the quantum correlations of the FR scenario but rather a common feature that arises in classical probability theory and causal inference
Pearl2009
where it often goes by the name of
collider bias
.

In the FR protocol, this feature is not immediately apparent as there are no settings in the actual protocol. However, as we have argued in the main text, these settings are implicit in the reasoning steps, and once made explicit, we see that there is indeed signalling under post-selection also in the FR scenario. Note that our general resolution and results do not depend on this aspect of signalling under post-selection or collider bias, although this is a feature of FR correlations. The general results show that imposing the
\mathbf{I}
assumption in a scenario where it fails is necessary for recovering FR type apparent apparent paradoxes (independently of this feature), and we have seen that
\mathbf{I}
does fail in the FR scenario.

This is also the case in our classical example, we can show that imposing
\mathbf{I}_{C}
also leads to an apparent paradox here, as the predictions do depend on the settings (the scenario violates
\mathbf{I}_{C}
). For instance, take
u=1
and
x_{2}=1
, then we have
a=k_{u}=1
and hence
\Lambda=x_{1}\oplus 1
, which gives
b=x_{1}\oplus x_{2}\oplus 1=x_{1}
. Therefore we have
P(b=0|x_{1}=0,u=1,x_{2}=1)=1
and
P(b=1|x_{1}=1,u=1,x_{2}=1)=1
. If we ignore the conditioning on the settings, as allowed by
\mathbf{I}_{C}
, we apparently have the paradoxical probability assignments
P(b=0|u=1)=1
and
P(b=1|u=1)=1
that correspond to
b
deterministically being
0
and
1
(both with certainty) whenever
u
is 1, which is an apparent violation of
S
as in the FR scenario. However we see that there is no real paradox once we correctly take into account all the settings and account for the correlations of the outcomes with the settings.

While the property of signalling under postselection holds in this simple classical circuit as it does in the quantum FR scenario, the exact correlations of the FR scenario are Bell non-local (or more generally, contextual) and cannot be reproduced using classical resources alone, once we fix the above circuit configuration where there is no information exchange between Alice and Bob’s sides (except the shared initial state). In order to reproduce the FR correlations classically, one must necessarily modify the circuit configuration to allow for additional connections such as allow the settings
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
to depend on the state preparation, or allow for causal connections between the
\matholdcal{A}
,
\matholdcal{U}
and
\matholdcal{B}
,
\matholdcal{W}
operations, analogous to superdeterministic or non-local hidden variable explanations of Bell correlations (see for instance
Wood2015
).
The correspondence between the FR argument and Hardy’s logical argument for contextuality of quantum correlations is discussed in the next section.

) corresponding to the entanglement version of the FR protocol. Here the boxes

M^{\textup{A}}_{unitary}
and

\{\pi^{A}_{x_{1}}\}
